({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
-module of the form

({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\mu}
for
\mu\in-P_{N}^{+}
contains a unique (up to nonzero scalar multiple) central element which we denote by
z_{2\mu}
. Moreover,
the set
\{z_{2\mu}|\ \mu\in-P^{+}_{N}\}
forms a basis for the center
Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
. This extends easily to

\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
with basis for the center equal to
\{z_{2\mu}|\ \mu\in-(P^{+}_{N}\times P^{+}_{N})\}
.

The arguments in
[
6
]
also apply to the
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
-modules of the form

({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\mu+c(\hat{\omega}_{N}/N)}
where
\mu\in w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{N}
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, the
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
-module

({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\mu+c\hat{\omega}_{N}/N}
for
\mu\in w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{N}
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
contains a unique (up to nonzero scalar multiple) central element of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
which we denote by
z_{2\mu+c(1/N)\hat{\omega}_{N}}
.
Moreover, it follows from (
42
) that

\displaystyle z_{2\mu+c(1/N)\hat{\omega}_{N}}=z_{2\mu}K_{(\hat{\omega}_{N})/N}^{c}

for all
\mu\in w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, the decomposition of the locally finite subalgebra in Theorem
7.1
ensures that the set
\{z_{2\mu}K_{(\hat{\omega}_{N})/N}^{c}|\ \mu\in w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{N},c\in\mathbb{Z}\}
forms a basis for the center,
Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))
, of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}).

Recall the decomposition of the locally finite part of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
given in Lemma
7.3
. The arguments in
[
6
]
also apply to the
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
-modules of the form

({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\mu+2c\hat{\omega}_{N}}
where
\mu\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, the
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
-module

({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\mu+2c\hat{\omega}_{N}}
for
\mu\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
contains a unique (up to nonzero scalar multiple) central element of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
which we denote by
z_{2\mu+2c\hat{\omega}_{N}}
.
Moreover, it follows from (
42
) that

\displaystyle z_{2\mu+2c\hat{\omega}_{N}}=z_{2\mu}K_{\hat{\omega}_{N}}^{2c}

for all
\mu\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}_{N}^{+}
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, the decomposition of the locally finite subalgebra of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
in Lemma
7.3
ensures that the set
\{z_{2\mu}K_{\hat{\omega}_{N}}^{2c}|\ \mu\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N},c\in\mathbb{N}\}
forms a basis for the center,
Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
, of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
in Types AI and AII. In the diagonal setting, the basis looks like

\{z_{2\mu}K_{\hat{\omega}_{n}}^{2c}K_{\hat{\omega}_{2n}}^{2c^{\prime}}\ \mu\in(w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N})\times(w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}),c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\}
. We focus here on Types AI and AII and use these observations to establish the same results in the diagonal case. Indeed, the results described so far in this section extend in a straightforward manner to the center of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{N})

with
P^{+}_{N}
replaced by
P^{+}_{N}\times P^{+}_{N}
,
w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
replaced by
w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}\times w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
,
c
replaced by a pair of
natural numbers

c
and
c^{\prime}
, and
K_{\hat{\omega}_{N}}^{c}
replaced by

K_{\hat{\omega}_{N}}^{c}K_{\hat{\omega}_{2N}}^{c^{\prime}}
.

The description of the basis for the center of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
combined with Lemma
7.3
and the subsequent discussion implies that

Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))=\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{C}(q)z_{2\lambda}

where
\lambda
runs over elements in
w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{n}
in Type AI,
w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{2n}
in Type AII and

w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{n}\times w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{n}
in the diagonal setting.

8.2.
Harish-Chandra maps

We start with the Harish-Chandra map defined for the simply connected quantized enveloping algebra
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
, a projection map based on a direct sum decomposition in
[
18
]
, Chapter 3. In particular, the Harish-Chandra map,
\varphi_{HC}
of the simply connected version
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})

is the projection onto the first component
\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
of the direct sum decomposition

(57)

\displaystyle\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})=\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})\oplus(G^{-}_{+}\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})+\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})U^{+}_{+}).

where
\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}),U^{+}_{+}
are defined in Section
2.2
and
G^{-}
is the subalgebra generated by
F_{i}K_{i},i=1,\dots,N-1
(with
G^{-}_{+}
its augmentation ideal) as defined in Section
7.3
.
(This is just
[
18
]
, (3.3) where the map
\varphi_{HC}
is called
\mathcal{P}
.)

Using the simply connected version of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
) introduced in Section
7.1
, we can extend the above decomposition to

(58)

\displaystyle\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})=\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})\oplus(G^{-}_{+}\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})+\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})U^{+}_{+})

where
\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
is equal to the Laurent polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}(q)[(K_{\hat{\omega}_{i}}K^{-1}_{(i/N)\hat{\omega}_{N}})^{\pm 1}|\ i=1,\cdots,N-1]
.
Note that this direct sum decomposition restricts to a direct sum decomposition on subalgebras of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
including ordinary quantized enveloping algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
, and more importantly, the special algebra
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
introduced in Section
7.3
:

(59)

\displaystyle{U}^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})={U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus(G^{-}_{+}U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})+{U}^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})U^{+}_{+}).

As explained in Section
7.3
,
{U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\ \lambda\in Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}]
. Note also that it is straightforward to write similar direct sum decompositions for the analogous algebras in the diagonal setting.

In each of the decompositions (
57
), (
58
), (
59
) for the algebra on the left side, we call the first summand its Cartan subalgebra.
We will denote the projection onto the Cartan subalgebra for each decomposition as the Harish-Chandra map
\varphi_{HC}
. We are using the same notation as for the first projection for the well studied
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
because all these decompositions lead to compatible projection maps due to obvious inclusions.

Recall that the restricted root system
\Sigma
is the root system with set of simple roots
\alpha_{1}^{\Sigma},\dots,\alpha^{\Sigma}_{n-1}
and fundamental weights
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n-1}
as described in detail in Section
2.5
.
Set

\check{\mathcal{A}}=\{K_{u}|\mu\in P_{\Sigma}\}.

This group is defined in Section 3 of
[
18
]
(see the top of page 24
in
[
18
]
). Write
\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]

for the group algebra of
\check{\mathcal{A}}
.
The Cartan subalgebra
\check{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
is a subset of the following direct sum decomposition (
[
18
]
, (3.5))

(60)

\displaystyle\check{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})\subseteq\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]\oplus\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{T}_{\theta}]_{+}

where
\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{T}_{\theta}]
is the group algebra associated to the group
\{K_{(\mu+\theta(\mu))/2)}|\ \mu\in P_{N}\}
. As in
[
18
]
(right after (3.5)), let
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
be the projection of
\check{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]
.

Note that this projection map can be described in the following
alternative way:

(61)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}K_{\mu}\mapsto\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}K_{\tilde{\mu}}

where
\mu
are elements in
P^{+}_{N}
, the
c_{\mu}
are scalars and
\tilde{\mu}
is the restricted weight defined by
\mu
.

Let
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
denote the projection of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]
defined by taking the composition of the Harish-Chandra map
\varphi_{HC}
with the projection
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
.
Note that
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
corresponds to
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\circ\mathcal{P}
of
[
18
]
(see beginning of
[
18
]
Section 6, especially Lemma 6.1). The map
\mathcal{P}
is the Harish-Chandra map for
the simply connected quantized enveloping algebra
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
in this reference.

It should also be noted that a more general restricted Harish-Chandra map is defined in
[
18
]
using a quantum analog of the Iwasawa decomposition. Upon restriction to the center, this more general restricted Harish-Chandra map agrees with
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
(see
[
18
]
, Lemma 6.1). Since we are only worried about the image of central elements under the restricted Harish-Chandra map, we just use
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
and do not consider the more general version.

Using the simply connected version of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
) introduced in Section
7.1
, we can extend the above decomposition to

(62)

\displaystyle\check{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})\subseteq\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})]\oplus\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})]\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{T}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})_{\theta}]_{+}

where
\check{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
equals the group generated by

•

K_{\hat{\eta}}
for
\eta\in\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{\Sigma}
and
K_{(1/N)\hat{\eta}_{n}}
for Type AI

•

K_{\hat{\eta}}
for
\eta\in\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{\Sigma}
and
K_{(1/N)\hat{\eta}_{2n}}
for Type AII

•

K_{\hat{\eta}}
for
\eta\in\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{\Sigma}
,
K_{(1/N)\hat{\eta}_{n}}
,
K_{(1/N)\hat{\eta}_{2n}}
in the diagonal case.

Also
\check{T}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})_{\theta}=\{K_{(\mu+\theta(\mu))/2}|\mu\in Q^{+}+w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}\}
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
denote the projection of the Cartan subalgebra
\check{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]
using the direct sum decomposition (
62
) and set
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\circ\varphi_{HC}
. Recall that
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
is a free module over
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
(see Section
7.1
) and the analogous result holds in the diagonal case. So
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
,
\varphi_{HC}
, and
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
restrict to projections by the same name for
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
and
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}\times\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
.

We can define a monoid that leads to a new polynomial ring. It is derived from the Cartan subalgebra of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

(notation from the beginning of Section
7.4
).
This algebra will be the main focus of the current section. Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{K_{2\tilde{\lambda}}|\ \lambda\in Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}\}

in Types AI and AII and

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{K_{2\tilde{\lambda}}|\ \lambda\in(Q^{+}_{N}\times Q^{+}_{N})+(w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}\times w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+})\}

in the diagonal case.
Another way to look at
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is to view it as a monoid generated by restricted root system partitions and the images of the
K_{i}^{2}
under restriction as well. This leads to the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]
, which by the description of the restricted weights for each type in Section
2.5
, satisfies

•

\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]=\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{i}},K_{2\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}|\ i=1,\dots,n-1]
for Type AI.

•

\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]=\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{i}},K_{2\tilde{\alpha}_{2i}}|\ i=1,\dots,n-1]
for Type AII.

•

\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]=\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{i}},K_{2\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}|\ i=1,\dots,n-1]
in the diagonal case.

Set
(T_{\theta})_{2}=\{K_{\lambda+\theta(\lambda)}|\ \lambda\in Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}\}
in Type AI and Type AII and

(T_{\theta})_{2}=\{K_{\lambda+\theta(\lambda)}|\ \lambda\in(Q^{+}_{N}\times Q^{+}_{N})+(w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}\times w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+})\}
in the diagonal case.
Recall that
\tilde{\lambda}=(\lambda-\theta(\lambda))/2
and so
K_{2\tilde{\lambda}}K_{(\lambda+\theta(\lambda))}=K_{2\lambda}
. It follows that

K_{2\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}(T_{\theta})_{2}
. Hence, we have the following inclusion

(63)

\displaystyle{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]\oplus\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]\mathbb{C}(q)[({T}_{\theta})_{2}]_{+}.

We define versions of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}

and the restricted Harish-Chandra map
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
associated to
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, keeping the notation from the simply connected versions above.
In particular, set
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
equal to the projection of
{U}^{0}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]
using (
63
). Just as for
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
, let
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
denote the projection of
U_{q}^{2}(\mathfrak{g})
onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[{\mathcal{A}}_{2}]
defined by taking the composition of the Harish-Chandra map
\varphi_{HC}
with the projection
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
. Once again, we can describe
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
using a version of (
61
) where in this case,
\mu
runs over elements in
Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}
for Types AI and AII and in
(Q^{+}_{N}\times Q^{+}_{N})+(w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}\times w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+})
for the diagonal setting.

Recall the description of the center of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
given at the end of Section
8.1
. It follows that its image in relation to the ordinary Harish-Chandra map is determined by

(64)

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu})={\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu^{\prime}})K_{\hat{{\omega}}_{N}}^{2s}

and, similarly, for the restricted Harish-Chandra map,

(65)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu})=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu^{\prime}})K_{\hat{{\omega}}_{N}}^{2s}=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu^{\prime}})K_{\hat{\eta}_{N}}^{2ms}

where
\mu\in-P^{+}_{N}
and
\mu^{\prime}\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
with
2\mu=2\mu^{\prime}+2s\hat{\omega}_{N}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover, the final equality in (
65
) follows from the equality on roots
m\hat{\eta}_{N}=\hat{\omega}_{N}
where
m=1
in Type AI and
m=2
for Type AII (see Section
2.5
).

Similar results holds in the diagonal setting. In particular, we have

(66)

\displaystyle\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\mu})=\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\mu^{\prime}})K_{\hat{\omega}_{n}}^{2s}K_{\hat{\omega}_{2n}}^{2s^{\prime}}

and

(67)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu})=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu^{\prime}})\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(K_{\hat{\omega}_{n}}^{2s}K_{\hat{\omega}_{2n}}^{2s^{\prime}})=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\mu^{\prime}})K_{\hat{\eta}_{n}}^{2(s+s^{\prime})}

where
\mu\in-P_{N}^{+}\times P^{+}_{N}
,
\mu^{\prime}\in w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{N}\times w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{N}
with
2\mu=2\mu^{\prime}+s\hat{\omega}_{n}+s^{\prime}\hat{\omega}_{n}
.
The final equality uses the fact that the restricted weights corresponding to
\hat{\omega}_{n}
and
\hat{\omega}_{2n}
are both equal to
\hat{\eta}_{n}
.

Note that for
z\in Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
we actually have
z\in U^{0}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus U^{-}_{+}U^{0}(\mathfrak{g})U^{+}_{+}
and so
z\cdot v=\varphi_{HC}(z)\cdot v
whenever
v
is a highest weight vector.
Now consider a highest weight generating vector
v_{2\beta}
for the simple module
L(2\beta)
where
\beta\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
. Since
\beta
is a restricted weight, it follows that
K_{2\mu}\cdot v_{2\beta}=K_{2\tilde{\mu}}\cdot v_{2\beta}
for all weights
\mu
. Hence

\displaystyle z\cdot v_{2\beta}=\varphi_{HC}(z)\cdot v_{2\beta}=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z)\cdot v_{2\beta}

for all
z\in Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
. Since
H_{2\beta}\in\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
is a highest weight vector of weight
2\beta
that generates a
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module isomorphic to
L(2\beta)
, we also have

(68)

\displaystyle z\cdot H_{2\beta}=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z)\cdot H_{2\beta}.

Since central elements act as scalars on all finite-dimensional simple
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules, it follows that the restricted Harish-Chandra map can be used to determine the eigenvalues with respect to the action of
Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
on
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
.

8.3.
Dotted Weyl group invariance

Let
\rho
denote the half sum of the positive roots for the root system associated to
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
and let
W
denote the Weyl group for this root system. Define a dotted Weyl group action on the Cartan subalgebra
\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
by (
[
18
]
, Chapter 3, (3.1):

\displaystyle w\circ q^{(\rho,\mu)}K_{\mu}=q^{(\rho,w\mu)}K_{w\mu}

Recall the following well-known result on the image of the center of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
under the ordinary Harish-Chandra map
\varphi_{HC}
:

Theorem 8.1
.

(
[
18
]
, Theorem 3.1, see also
[
6
]
, Lemma 7.17 and 7.1.25) The ordinary Harish-Chandra map
\varphi_{HC}
defines an isomorphism from
Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))

onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\lambda\in P_{N}]^{W\circ}
.

Note that terms of the form

(69)

\displaystyle m_{2\lambda}=\sum_{w\in W}q^{(\rho,2w\lambda)}K_{2w\lambda}

for
\lambda\in P_{N}^{+}

form a basis for the dotted Weyl invariant elements in
\check{U}^{0}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
.
There will be times that it is useful to rewrite the above formula using the lowest weight term
w_{0}\lambda
. In particular, the above formula is equal to

\displaystyle m_{2\lambda}=m_{2w_{0}\lambda}=\sum_{w\in W}q^{(\rho,ww_{0}\lambda)}K_{2ww_{0}\lambda}.

Now the diagonal case is not discussed in
[
18
]
. However,
it is well-known and straightforward to check that Theorem
8.1
holds for the simply connected quantized enveloping algebras of semisimple Lie algebras such as
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{n}).
In this case, the basis of dotted Weyl invariant elements takes the same form as above with only difference being
\lambda\in P^{+}_{N}\times P^{+}_{N}
.

Set
\tilde{\rho}=(\rho-\theta(\rho))/2
, the restricted weight associated to
\rho
. The dotted action of
W_{\Sigma}
on elements of

\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]
is given by
the following formula from
[
18
]
, p.24 of Chapter 3:

(70)

\displaystyle w\bullet q^{(\tilde{\rho},\mu)}K_{2\gamma}=q^{(\tilde{\rho},w\gamma)}K_{2w\gamma}

for all
w\in W_{\Sigma}
and
\gamma\in P_{\Sigma}
. Moreover, by
[
18
]
Lemma 3.2, given an element
\tilde{w}\in W_{\Sigma}
, there exists
w\in W
so that the restriction of
w
to
\Sigma
equals
\tilde{w}
.
Thus terms of the form

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2\lambda}=\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{(\tilde{\rho},2w\lambda)}K_{2w\lambda}

with
\lambda\in w_{0}P^{+}_{\Sigma}
are dotted
W_{\Sigma}
invariants.

Noting that elements of the center
Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
are invariant under the ordinary dotted Weyl group action (Theorem
8.1
above) yields the following version of
[
18
]
, Chapter 3, Theorem 3.3. Recall the definition of
\check{\mathcal{A}}
(see Section
8.2
) and define the related group
\mathcal{A}
by

\mathcal{A}=\{K_{2u}|\mu\in P_{\Sigma}\}

(see
[
18
]
, middle of page 25).

Theorem 8.2
.

Given
f\in Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
,
the element
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(f)

is a dotted
W_{\Sigma}
invariant element of

\mathbb{C}(q)[\check{\mathcal{A}}]
. Moreover,
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
is a subring of
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{W_{\Sigma}\bullet}.

Theorem
8.2
also extends to the diagonal case.
First note that the root system in this case consists of the disjoint union of the root systems for each copy of
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
. This leads to two half sums of the positive roots:
\rho_{1}
for the first copy and
\rho_{2}
for the second.
It is also straightforward to check that elements of
W_{\Sigma}
lift to elements of
W\times W
, the Weyl group for the root system of
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle w_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}=w_{\alpha_{i}}w_{\alpha_{n+i}}{\rm\ and\ }w_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n+i}}=w_{\alpha_{n+i}}w_{\alpha_{i}}

for each reflection associated to the simple restricted roots
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
or
\tilde{\alpha}_{n+i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n-1
in both cases.

It follows from (
69
) that sums taken over
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in P^{+}_{N}\times P^{+}_{N}

\displaystyle m_{(2\lambda_{1},2\lambda_{2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in W\times W}q^{((\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),(2w_{1}\lambda_{1},2w_{2}\lambda_{2})}K_{(2w_{1}\lambda_{1},2w_{2}\lambda_{2})}

form a basis for the dotted invariant elements for images of the center of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{n})
with respect to the ordinary Harish-Chandra map. Moreover, we can write this as a product with two factors:

\displaystyle m_{(2\lambda_{1},2\lambda_{2})}=m_{2\lambda_{1}}m_{2\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=(\sum_{w_{1}\in W}q^{(\rho_{1},2w_{1}\lambda_{1})}K_{2w_{1}\lambda_{1}})(\sum_{w_{2}\in W}q^{(\rho_{2},2w_{2}\lambda_{2})}K_{2w_{2}\lambda_{2}})

Note that
w_{1}
is a product of reflections with respect to the roots
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}
and
w_{2}
is a product of reflections with respect to
\alpha_{n+1},\dots,\alpha_{2n-1}
. Set
\tilde{w}_{1}
equal to the product where each
w_{\alpha_{i}}
is replaced by
w_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}\in W_{\Sigma}
. It follows that
\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}=w_{1}\lambda_{1}.
We can define
\tilde{w}_{2}
in a similar fashion. Thus we can rewrite the formula for
m_{(2\lambda_{1},2\lambda_{2})}
as

\displaystyle m_{2\lambda_{1}}m_{2\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=(\sum_{\tilde{w}_{1}\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{(\rho_{1},2\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1})}K_{2\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}})(\sum_{\tilde{w}_{2}\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{(\rho_{2},2\tilde{w}_{2}\lambda_{2})}K_{2\tilde{w}_{2}\lambda_{2}})

Recall that the inner product on the restricted root system in the diagonal case satisfies
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Sigma}=2(\cdot,\cdot).

On the other hand,
\tilde{\rho}_{1}=\tilde{\rho}_{2}=(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})/2=\rho_{\Sigma}
.
For
i=1,\dots,n-1
, we have
w_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}\lambda_{1}=w_{{\alpha}_{i}}\lambda_{1}
and
w_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}\theta(\lambda_{1})=w_{\alpha_{i+n}}\theta(\lambda_{1})=\theta(w_{\alpha_{i}}\lambda_{1})=\theta(w_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}\lambda_{1}).
Hence
\tilde{w}_{1}\theta(\lambda_{1})=\theta(\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1})
. Note that this guarantees that
(\rho_{1},\theta(\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}))=0

since
\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}
is in the first copy of
P_{N}
and so

\theta(\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1})
must be in the second.
Hence

(\rho_{1},2\tilde{w}_{1}{\lambda}_{1})=(\rho_{1},2(\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}-\theta(\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}))=(\rho_{1},4\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})=(\tilde{\rho}_{1},4\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})=(\rho_{\Sigma},2\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})_{\Sigma}.

Thus we can express
m_{2\lambda_{1}}
as

\displaystyle m_{2\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=(\sum_{\tilde{w}_{1}\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{(\tilde{\rho}_{1},2\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})_{\Sigma}}K_{2(\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}}K_{(2\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}+2\theta(\tilde{w}\lambda_{1}))/2)})

\displaystyle=(\sum_{\tilde{w}_{1}\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{\rho_{\Sigma},2\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})_{\Sigma}}K_{2(\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})}(K_{(2\tilde{w}_{1}\lambda_{1}+2\theta(\tilde{w_{1}}\lambda_{1}))/2}-1)+(\sum_{\tilde{w}_{1}\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{({\rho}_{\Sigma},2\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})_{\Sigma}}K_{2(\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})}).

Hence,
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(m_{\lambda_{1}})=\sum_{\tilde{w}_{1}\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{(\tilde{\rho}_{1},2\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})_{\Sigma}}K_{2(\tilde{w}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1})}
and a similar result holds for
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(m_{\lambda_{2}})
. Both of these formulas are dotted
W_{\Sigma}
invariant and hence the diagonal case also satisfies the conclusion of Theorem
8.2
.

We can translate Theorem
8.1
and Theorem
8.2
to the setting of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
where
\mathfrak{sl}
is replaced by
\mathfrak{gl}
everywhere as follows.
Recall the isomorphism of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
-modules, the first generated by
K_{2\lambda}
and the second by
K_{2\lambda^{\prime}}
where

\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\lambda_{i}w_{0}\omega_{i}
and
\lambda^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\lambda_{i}w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}

as given in (
48
). From the description of the isomorphism in (
49
) including the paragraph below this formula, we see that
K_{2\lambda}K_{2\lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}
is in the center of
\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
.
The diagonal case is very similar where here we express
\lambda=\lambda_{(n)}+\lambda_{(2n)}
where
\lambda_{(n)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\lambda_{i}w_{0}\omega_{i}
and
\lambda_{(2n)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\lambda_{i+n}w_{0}\omega_{i+n}
. Similarly define
\lambda^{\prime}
with
w_{0}\omega_{i}
replaced by
w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}
for each
i
.

Theorem 8.3
.

We have the following analogs of Theorem
8.1
and Theorem
8.2
for the algebra
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
:

(i)

The ordinary Harish-Chandra map
\varphi_{HC}
defines an isomorphism from
Z({U}^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))

onto the dotted
W
invariants
\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\ \lambda\in Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}]^{W\circ}
in Types AI and AII and onto the dotted
W
invariants

\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\ \lambda\in(Q^{+}_{N}\times Q^{+}_{N})+(w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}\times w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N})]^{W\circ}
in the diagonal setting.

(ii)

Given
f\in Z({U}^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
,
the element
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(f)

is a dotted
W_{\Sigma}
invariant element of

\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\ \lambda\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}+w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{\Sigma}].
Moreover,
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z({U}^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
is a subring of
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]^{W_{\Sigma}\bullet}.

Proof.

Recall that the ordinary Harish-Chandra map is defined via (
59
) as the projection onto the Cartan subalgebra of
U_{q}^{2}(\mathfrak{g})
. This Cartan subalgebra is equal to

\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\ \lambda\in Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}]
in Types AI and AII and
\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\lambda}|\ \lambda\in(Q^{+}_{N}\times Q^{+}_{N})+(w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}\times w_{0}{\Lambda}^{+}_{N})]
in the diagonal setting. Note that the diagonal case follows from the other two so our focus is entirely on the singleton setting.

Observe that
(G^{-}_{+}U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})+{U}^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})U^{+}_{+})
is a two-sided ideal in
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
since
{U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})G^{-}_{+}=G^{-}_{+}{U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})
and
U^{+}_{+}{U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})={U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})U^{+}_{+}
. It follows that the ordinary Harish-Chandra map defines an algebra homomorphism onto
{U}_{2}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})
. Therefore
\varphi_{HC}
restricts to an algebra homomorphism on the subalgebra
Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
. We show below that this map is injective and hence an isomorphism as stated in
Theorem
8.3
(i).

Now
\mathcal{F}(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
can be written as a direct sum of simple ad-invariant left coideals of the form
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))K_{2\lambda}
where
\lambda\in w_{0}P^{+}_{N}
(see (
41
)). On the other hand, the same type of
decomposition for
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
in Theorem
7.3
gives us

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}}({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\lambda^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{F}(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N},s\in\mathbb{N}}({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2\lambda^{\prime}+2s\hat{\omega}_{N}}.

(Note that
w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}_{N}^{+}+\mathbb{N}\hat{\omega}_{N}=w_{0}\Lambda_{N}^{+}
.)
As explained right before this theorem,
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))(K_{2\lambda})
is isomorphic to
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))(K_{2\lambda^{\prime}})
via the map sending
({\rm ad}\ a)\cdot(K_{2\lambda})
to
({\rm ad}\ a)\cdot(K_{2\lambda^{\prime}})
for all
a\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
. With respect to this map, the central element
z_{2\lambda}
of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))(K_{2\lambda})
is sent to the central element
z_{2\lambda^{\prime}}
of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))(K_{2\lambda})
.
Now
\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\lambda})
is dotted Weyl invariant. It follows that
\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\lambda^{\prime}})
is also dotted Weyl invariant.

Note that
\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\lambda^{\prime}})
is an element of the Cartan subalgebra
\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2(\lambda^{\prime}+\gamma)}|\ \gamma\in Q^{+}_{N}{\rm\ and\ }\lambda^{\prime}\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}].
Moreover,
Q^{+}_{N}
does not contain any anti-dominant integral weights and so the only anti-dominant weights come from
w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}_{N}^{+}
. By Theorem
8.1
,
\{\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\lambda})|\ \lambda\in w_{0}P^{+}_{N}\}
forms a basis for the dotted invariants of
\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\gamma}|\gamma\in P_{N}]
.
Hence,
\{\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\lambda^{\prime}})|\ \lambda^{\prime}\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}\}
forms a basis for the dotted invariants of
\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\gamma}|\gamma\in Q^{+}_{N}+w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}]^{W\circ}
. Since
\varphi_{HC}(z_{2\lambda^{\prime}})
is an element of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}}
, the only possible dotted Weyl invariant element (up to nonzero scalar) is

\displaystyle m_{2\lambda^{\prime}}=m_{2w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}}=\sum_{w\in W}q^{(\rho,2ww_{0}\lambda^{\prime})}K_{2ww_{0}\lambda^{\prime}}.

We get a similar equality for
m_{2w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}+2s\hat{w}_{N}}

\displaystyle m_{2w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}+2s\hat{\omega}_{N}}=\sum_{w\in W}q^{(\rho,2ww_{0}\lambda^{\prime})}K_{2ww_{0}\lambda^{\prime}}K_{\hat{\omega}_{N}}^{2s}

for
w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}\in w_{0}\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}.

Note that these elements span the image of the center of
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Moreover, these terms are linearly independent since they each take the form

\displaystyle m_{2\lambda^{\prime}+2s\hat{\omega}_{N}}\in(q^{(\rho,2w_{0}\lambda^{\prime})}K_{2w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}}+\sum_{\beta>w_{0}\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{C}(q)K_{2\beta})(K_{2\hat{\omega}_{N}})^{s}.

Here the inequality below the summation sign refers to the partial order defined by
\beta>\gamma
provided
\beta-\gamma\in Q^{+}_{N}
. This finishes the proof for Theorem
8.3
(i).

The proof of (ii) is similar to that of (i). In fact,

\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a projection onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]
with kernel the two-sided ideal
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]\mathbb{C}(q)[({T}_{\theta})_{2}]_{+}
inside
the right hand side of (
63
). Hence the restricted Harish-Chandra map is an algebra homomorphism of
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]
onto itself and

\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})))
is a subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]
. By the discussion preceding the lemma relating
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
and by Theorem
8.1
, this image of
Z(U_{q}^{2}(\mathfrak{g}))
under
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
is invariant under the dotted action of
W_{\Sigma}
.
∎

8.4.
Central generators

In
[
18
]
, it is shown that
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
is isomorphic to the entire ring of invariants
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{\bullet}
. Indeed we have the following version of
[
18
]
, Theorem 8.1.

Theorem 8.4
.

Given a symmetric pair of Type AI, Type AII, or of diagonal type, the image under the restricted Harish-Chandra map
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))

is isomorphic to the dotted
W_{\Sigma}
invariants,
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{\bullet}
, of
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]
.

Recall that
\mathcal{A}=\{K_{2\mu}|\ \mu\in P_{\Sigma}\}
. Hence
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{\bullet}
is a polynomial ring in variables

m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}}
,
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Thus we must show that
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))

contains these generators. We start with an overview of the proof and then fill in more of the details below. Eventually we will obtain the analog result for
Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
. Consider central elements defined by

z_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}}{\rm\ for\ }i=1,\dots,n-1

where
z_{2w_{0}\omega_{j}}
is the central element of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))\cdot K_{2w_{0}\omega_{j}}
for
Types AI and AII. For the diagonal case, set

z_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}}{\rm\ and\ }z_{i+n}=z_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}}{\rm\ for\ }i=1,\dots,n-1.

In this case the
z_{i}
is the central element of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{n}))\cdot K_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}}
and
z_{i+n}
is the central element of
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{n}))\cdot K_{2w_{0}\omega_{i+n}}.
The argument for Theorem

8.4
in Type AI simply shows that
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{i})=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. The diagonal case is similar. On the other hand,
the proof for Type AII is more difficult. It involves an inductive argument that first establishes
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{j}}\in\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
for all
j<k
and then realizes
m^{\Sigma}_{2w\eta_{k}}
as a linear combination of
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{k}))
plus products
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}}
for
j<k
and
i<k
.

Proof of Theorem
8.4
, Type AI and Diagonal Type:
Note that
\tilde{\omega}_{i}=\eta_{i}
, the fundamental restricted weight, for
i=1,\dots,n-1
in both Types. (We can use either
z_{i}
or
z_{n+i}
in the diagonal case.) Since the restricted root system is of Type
A_{n-1}
, the fundamental weights
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n-1}
are minuscule.
In other words,
\eta_{j}\not>_{\Sigma}\eta_{k}
for any pair
j,k
and, in addition,
\eta_{j}\not>_{\Sigma}0
for any
j
where the inequalities are defined via the partial order:
\beta>_{\Sigma}\gamma
provided
\beta-\gamma\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}
for
\beta,\gamma\in P^{+}_{\Sigma}
.
Recall that
z_{2\mu}
is the unique up to nonzero scalar central element in
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))K_{2\mu}
for
\mu\in w_{0}P^{+}_{\Sigma}
.
When
\mu=w_{0}{\omega}_{i}
for the two families Type AI and diagonal type under consideration, the image of
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}})
is in

(71)

\displaystyle K_{2w_{0}\tilde{\omega}_{i}}+\sum_{\beta\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}}\mathbb{C}(q)K_{2w_{0}\tilde{\omega}_{i}+2\beta}=K_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}}+\sum_{\beta\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}}\mathbb{C}(q)K_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}+2\beta}

up to a nonzero scalar.
Since this element is dotted invariant with respect to the restricted root system, it follows that
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}})={m}^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}}
for each
i
. Thus
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,n-1
are a set of generators for
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{\bullet}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.4
, Type AII:

Note
that in Section
2.5
in the final sentence on Type AII, we see that
\tilde{\omega}_{1}=\eta_{1}
.
This is the same as part of
[
18
]
, Lemma 2.4.

Lemma 8.5
.

(
[
18
]
, Lemma 2.4 (i) applied to Type AII) The first fundamental weight
\omega_{1}
in
P^{+}_{N}
restricts to the first fundamental restricted weight
\tilde{\omega}_{1}=\eta_{1}
.

Since
\eta_{1}
is minuscule and
\tilde{\omega}_{1}=\eta_{1}
, we can use the same argument as used for minuscule fundamental restricted weights for Type AI and the diagonal type. In particular
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{1}})=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{1}}
and so
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{1}}\in\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{\bullet}.

The next lemma is key in establishing
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k}}\in\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{\bullet}
for
k>1
.

Lemma 8.6
.

(
[
18
]
, Lemma 8.8) Assume Type AII with
\Sigma
of Type
A_{n-1}
.

(i)

Let
k
be a positive integer such that
1\leq 2k\leq n
. Then
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k}}
is in the span of the set
\{\tilde{\varphi}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{2k}})\}\cup\{m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+j}}|\ 0\leq j<k\}
.

(ii)

Let
k
be a positive integer such that
1\leq 2k+1\leq n
. Then
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k+1}}
is in the span of the set
\{\tilde{\varphi}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{2k+1}})\}\cup\{m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+1+j}}|\ 0\leq j<k\}
.

We follow the proof of
[
18
]
, Theorem 8.9 which is the Type AII part of Theorem
8.4
of this paper. Set
R=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(\check{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N}))
.
We already showed that
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{1}}=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\eta_{1}})
is in
R
. The strategy is to use induction based on the assumption that
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}}\in R
for
1\leq i<j
. Assume first that
j
is even, say
j=2k
. By the inductive hypothesis, both
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k-i}}
and
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+i}}
are in
R
for
1\leq i<k
. Hence
R
contains all the products
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k-i}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+i}}
for
1\leq i<k
. By Lemma
8.6
(i),
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k}}
is in
R
. A similar induction argument using (ii) applies to
j=2k+1
and shows that
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k+1}}\in R
. Thus by induction,
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{j}}
is in
R
for
j=1
(the minuscule element),
j
takes on all even values between
2
and
n-1
by (i), and
j
takes on all odd integers between
3
and
n-1
by (ii).

∎

Note that a consequence of the above result is that
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}]^{W_{\Sigma}\bullet}
is a polynomial ring in the variables
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{1}),\dots,\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{n-1}).
The next theorem obtains similar results for
Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
. First, we need to define analogs of the
z_{i}
that rely on partitions in
\hat{\Lambda}^{+}_{N}
instead of the weight lattice
P^{+}_{N}
. In particular, given
w_{0}\omega_{i}\in w_{0}P^{+}_{N}
with
z_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\omega_{i}}
, set
\hat{z}_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}}
. In other words, the central elements listed above in (i), (ii), (iii) are converted to elements
\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{n-1}
. There is also an extra generator corresponding to the element

z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{n}}=z_{2\hat{\omega}_{n}}
. Explicitly, define the central elements
\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{n}
by

(i)

\hat{z}_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}}=z_{2(\epsilon_{n+1-i}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})}
for
i=1,\dots,n
in Type AI and the diagonal case.

(ii)

\hat{z}_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}}=z_{2(\epsilon_{2n+1-i}+\cdots+\epsilon_{2n})}
for
i=1,\dots,n
in Type AII.

(iii)

\hat{z}_{i}=z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}}=z_{2(\epsilon_{n+1-i}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})}
and
\hat{z}_{i+n}=z_{2(\epsilon_{2n+1-i}+\cdots+\epsilon_{2n})}
for
i=1,\dots,n
in the diagonal type.

Recall that
Q_{N}\cap w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{N}=0
(see Section
7.3
). The same holds for restricted root systems. In particular,
Q_{\Sigma}\cap w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}=0
.

Theorem 8.7
.

The algebra
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})))
equals
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]^{W_{\Sigma}\bullet}

and is the polynomial ring on the
n
variables
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{1}),\dots,\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{n}).

Proof.

Consider Type AI. Recall that
z_{2\mu}
is the unique (up to nonzero scalar) central element in
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))K_{2\mu}
. When
\mu=\hat{\omega}_{n}
, the element
K_{2\hat{\omega}_{n}}
is a central element. Thus we have
z_{2\hat{\omega}_{n}}=K_{2\hat{\omega}_{n}}
. Hence
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\hat{\omega}_{n}})=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\circ{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2\hat{\omega}_{n}})=\sum_{w\in W}q^{(\rho,w\hat{\eta})}K_{2w\hat{\eta}_{n}}=q^{(\rho,w\hat{\eta})}|W|K_{2\hat{\eta}_{n}}
in Type AI. Thus the algebra generated by
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{i}),

i=1,\dots,n
in Type AI contains
K_{2\hat{\eta}_{n}}
.
A similar statement holds in the diagonal case. In this case, we can use either the algebra generated by
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n
or the algebra generated by
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{i+n})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Both produce the same algebra and that algebra contains
K_{2\hat{\eta}_{n}}
.

As explained above, the fundamental weights
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n-1}
are minuscule. Note that the same is true for the fundamental partitions
\hat{\eta}_{1},\dots,\hat{\eta}_{n-1}
.
Moreover, we have the analog of (
71
) in the partition setting. Namely,
as in the proof of Theorem
8.4
for Type AI and the diagonal type,

\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}})

is an element of

(72)

\displaystyle K_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{i}}+\sum_{\beta\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}}\mathbb{C}(q)K_{2w_{0}{\hat{\eta}}_{i}+2\beta_{i}}=K_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{i}}+\sum_{\beta\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}}\mathbb{C}(q)K_{2w_{0}\eta_{i}+2\beta}

up to a nonzero scalar. By the discussion preceding the theorem,
Q^{+}_{\Sigma}\cap w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}=0
.
Since
z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{i}}
is central, its image under
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
must be dotted invariant with respect to
W_{\Sigma}
. By (
72
), the only possible dotted invariant element is
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{i}}
up to a nonzero scalar. Hence the theorem holds in Type AI and the diagonal setting.

Now consider Type AII. As explained in the proof of Theorem
8.4
for Type AII, the first fundamental restricted root
\eta_{1}
is minuscule. Hence, arguing as above, we have

\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{1})=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{1}}.

By Lemma
8.6
,
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k}}
can be written as a linear combination of elements in the set

\displaystyle\{\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{2k}}),m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+j}}|\ 1\leq j<k\}

for
1<2k\leq n
. Similarly,
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k+1}}
can be written as a linear combination of elements in the set

\displaystyle\{\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{2k+1}}),m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+1-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+j}}|\ 1\leq j<k\}

for
1<2k+1\leq n
.

We recall here some formulas involving restricted weights from Section
2.5
.
In particular,

w_{0}\omega_{j}=w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{j}-(j/2n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}
. As explained in Section
2.5
,
2\hat{\eta}_{n}=\hat{\omega}_{2n}
and so
w_{0}\eta_{j}=w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{j}-(j/n)\hat{\eta}_{n}
for
j=1,\dots,n-1
.
Hence

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{j}})=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{j}})K_{(-j/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}

for each
j=1,\dots,n-1
.
Thus we also have

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{s}}=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{s}}K_{(-s/n)2\hat{\eta}_{2n}}=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{s}}K_{(-s/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}

for each
s=1,\dots,n-1
and so

(73)

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}{\eta}_{k+j}}

\displaystyle=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+j}}K_{((-k+j)/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}K_{((-k-j)/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}

(74)

\displaystyle=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+j}}K_{(-2k/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}

for each
k
and each
j
with
1\leq j<k
.
Similarly,

(75)

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}{\eta}_{k+1+j}}

\displaystyle=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+1+j}}K_{((-k+j)/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}K_{((-k-1-j)/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}

(76)

\displaystyle=m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+1+j}}K_{((-2k-1)/n)\hat{\omega}_{2n}}

for each
j
with
1\leq j<k
.
Thus a relation of the form

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{2k}}=a_{0}\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\omega_{2k}})+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}a_{j}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\eta_{k+j}}

becomes

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{2k}}K_{(-2k/{n})\hat{\eta}_{2n}}=a_{0}\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{2k}})K_{(-2k/n)\hat{\eta}_{2n}}+\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}a_{j}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+j}}K_{({-2k}/n)\hat{\eta}_{2n}}.

Multiplying both sides by
K_{({2k/n)\hat{\eta}_{2n}}}
yields

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{2k}}=a_{0}\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{2k}})+\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}a_{j}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+j}}.

Hence

m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{2k}}
can be written as a linear combination of elements in the set

(77)

\displaystyle\{\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{2k}}),m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+j}}|\ 1\leq j<k\}

for
1<2k\leq n
.
A similar argument shows that

m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{2k+1}}
can be written as a linear combination of elements in the set

(78)

\displaystyle\{\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z_{2w_{0}\hat{\omega}_{2k+1}}),m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k-j}}m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{k+1+j}}|\ 1\leq j<k\}

for
1<2k+1\leq n
.

Note that when either
2k=n
or
2k+1=n
we see that
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{n}}
is a linear combination of elements in the set (
77
) or (
78
) depending on the parity of
n
.
Thus arguing by induction as in the proof of Theorem
8.4
, Type AII, the algebra generated by
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(\hat{z}_{i}),

i=1,\dots,n
contains the elements
m^{\Sigma}_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{j}}
,
j=1,\dots,n
. The theorem follows from the facts that

w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{n}=\hat{\eta}_{n}
,
K_{2\hat{\eta}_{n}}
is invariant with respect to the dotted action of
W_{\Sigma}
and, thus,
m_{2w_{0}\hat{\eta}_{n}}
is a nonzero scalar multiple of
K_{2\hat{\eta}_{n}}
.
∎

Let
Z
denote the subring of
Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
generated by the elements
\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{n}
. By the previous theorem, Theorem
8.7
, the image under the restricted Harish-Chandra map
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
of the algebra generated by
\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{n}
is a polynomial ring with these variables. Hence, since
Z
is commutative,
\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{n}
must also generate a polynomial ring of rank
n
. We see that the same is true for the image of
Z
with respect to
\Upsilon
of
Corollary
7.5
.

Corollary 8.8
.

The algebra
\Upsilon(Z)
is a polynomial subring of
{\rm End}\ \mathscr{P}_{\theta}
with variables
\Upsilon(\hat{z}_{i}),i=1,\dots,n
. Moreover, each
\Upsilon(\hat{z}_{r})
is an element in
\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}
of degree less than or equal to
2r
.

Proof.

Recall that
m^{\Sigma}_{2\lambda}=\sum_{w\in W_{\Sigma}}q^{(\tilde{\rho},2w\lambda)}K_{2w\lambda}
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}.
Note that
\tilde{\gamma}\in Q^{+}
for all
\gamma\in Q^{+}
. Hence, given
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
and
w\in W_{\Sigma}
, we have
w\lambda\in\lambda-Q^{+}
. Since
(\rho,\gamma)
is a positive integer for
\gamma\in Q^{+}
so is
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\gamma})=(\rho,\tilde{\gamma})
. Hence, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2\lambda}\in q^{(\tilde{\rho},2\lambda)}K_{2\lambda}+\sum_{\gamma\in Q^{+}}q^{{(\tilde{\rho},2\lambda)}-2}\mathbb{C}[q^{-2}]K_{2\lambda-2\gamma}.

Let
v_{2\beta}
be a highest weight generating vector for
L(2\beta)
where
\beta\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
.
It follows that

(79)

\displaystyle m^{\Sigma}_{2\lambda}\cdot v_{2\beta}\in\left(q^{(\tilde{\rho}+2\beta,2\lambda)}(1+q^{-2}\mathbb{C}[q^{-2}])\right)v_{2\beta}.

Note that any
a\in\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]^{W_{\Sigma}\bullet}
can be expressed as a linear combination
a_{1}m^{\Sigma}_{2\lambda_{1}}+\cdots+a_{s}m^{\Sigma}_{2\lambda_{s}}
where each
\lambda_{s}\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
.
We argue that there exists
\beta\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
so that
a\cdot v_{2\beta}\neq 0
. Reordering and multiplying by a nonzero element of
\mathbb{C}[q]
if necessary, we may assume that
|\lambda_{1}|\geq|\lambda_{j}|
for each
2\leq j\leq s
,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[q]
for each
1\leq i\leq s
, and
a_{1}=q^{d}+
terms of lower degree in
q
.
It is straightforward to check that there exists
\beta_{1}\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
such that
(\lambda_{1},\beta_{1})>(\lambda,\beta_{1})
for all
\lambda\neq\lambda_{1}
satisfying
|\lambda|\leq|\lambda_{1}|
. Thus by (
79
), for a large enough positive integer
r
,
a\cdot v_{2r\beta}=q^{d+(\tilde{\rho}+2r\beta_{1},2\lambda_{1})}+
terms of degree strictly less than
d+(\tilde{\rho}+2r\beta_{1},2\lambda_{1})
. Thus
a\cdot v_{2\beta}\neq 0
for
\beta=r\beta_{1}
.

Recall that
H_{2\beta}
is a highest weight vector in the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
for each
\beta\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
. By (
68
),
z\cdot H_{2\beta}=\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z)\cdot H_{2\beta}
for all
z\in Z(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
. By Theorem
8.7
,
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}
defines an isomorphism from
Z
onto
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathcal{A}_{2}]^{W_{\Sigma}\bullet}
. Hence, by the previous paragraph, given
z\in Z
, we can find
\beta\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}
so that
\tilde{\varphi}_{HC}(z)\cdot H_{2\beta}\neq 0
. It follows that
z\cdot H_{2\beta}\neq 0
. By Corollary
7.5
,
\Upsilon(z)\cdot H_{2\beta}=z\cdot H_{2\beta}
and so
\Upsilon(z)\neq 0
. In other words,
\Upsilon
is injective upon restriction to
Z
. This proves the first assertion of this corollary.

For the second assertion, note that

\hat{z}_{r}
is in the
({\rm ad}\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
-module generated by
K_{2\epsilon_{N+1-r}+\cdots+2\epsilon_{N}}
where
N=n
in Type AI and the diagonal case and
N=2n
in Type AII. Hence, by Proposition
6.3
,
\Upsilon(\hat{z}_{r})
has degree less than or equal to

2r
. ∎

We return to this degree computation in Section
9.3
. Indeed, Theorem
9.6
establishes the equality
\deg(\Upsilon(\hat{z}_{r}))=2r
.

9.
Quantum Capelli operators

9.1.
Definition and description

The decompositions in Section
4.4
combined together yield the following module decomposition and related isomorphisms of left
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules:

(80)

\displaystyle\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}=\bigoplus_{\mu,\xi\in\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}}\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H_{2\mu}\right)\otimes\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H^{*}_{2\xi}\right)\cong\bigoplus_{\mu,\xi\in\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}}L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\xi)

where
L^{*}(2\xi)
is the left
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module dual of
L(2\xi)
.
This last isomorphism can be made concrete by sending
H_{2\mu}
to a pre-chosen highest weight generating vector
v_{2\mu}
of
L(2\mu)
. Similarly,
H^{*}_{2\xi}
is sent to
a nonzero scalar multiple of
v_{2\xi}^{*}
,
the lowest weight generating vector for
L^{*}(2\xi)
satisfying
v^{*}_{2\xi}(v_{2\xi})=1
. This nonzero scalar is determined in the next lemma using the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
defined by (
23
) in Section
5.2
.

Recall that there is a natural isomorphism from
(L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\mu))
to
{\rm End}\ L(2\mu)
as left
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules and so
(L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\mu))^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}
is the one-dimensional subspace consisting of the scalars. Furthermore,
(L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\xi))^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}=0
for
\mu\neq\xi
.
Hence, the left
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module invariants of
\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}\cong\bigoplus_{\mu,\xi\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}}(L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\xi))^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}}(L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\mu))^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}.

Let
C_{\mu}
be the basis vector for the space
\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H_{2\mu}\right)\otimes\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H^{*}_{2\mu}\right)^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}
corresponding to the identity element in

{\rm End}\ L(2\mu)
via the isomorphism between
\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H_{2\mu}\right)\otimes\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H^{*}_{2\mu}\right)
and
L(2\mu)\otimes L^{*}(2\mu)
described above.
We refer to the set

\{C_{\mu}|\ \mu\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{+}\}
as the Capelli operators. Note that the Capelli operators form a basis for the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

invariant subspace of
\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}
.
Since
C_{\mu}\in\left((U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H_{2\mu})\otimes(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H^{*}_{2\mu})\right)
, it follows that
C_{\mu}
has degree
2|\mu|
in terms of the filtration
\mathcal{J}
. In the lemma below, we drop the tensor product notation and simply right
H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\mu}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

The Capelli operator
C_{\mu}
for
\mu\in\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}
lies in

(81)

\displaystyle\langle H_{2\mu}^{*},H_{2\mu}\rangle^{-1}H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\mu}+(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})(U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*}).

Moreover
C_{\mu}\cdot H_{2\mu}=H_{2\mu}
and
C_{\mu}\cdot H_{2{\lambda}}=0
for
|\mu|\geq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
.

Proof.

Recall that
H_{2\mu}
is a highest weight vector of weight
2\mu
and
H_{2\mu}^{*}
is a lowest weight vector
of weight
-2\mu
. Hence

C_{\mu}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot(H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\mu})\subseteq(U^{-}\cdot H_{2\mu})(U^{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*}).

Using the augmentation ideals
U^{-}_{+}
and
U^{+}_{+}
, this simplifies to

\displaystyle C_{\mu}\in\gamma H_{2\mu}H_{2\mu}^{*}+(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})(U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*})

for some scalar
\gamma
. It follows that (
81
) is true up to the scalar in front of
H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\mu}
.

The projection map
\pi
defined in Section
5.2
can be used to understand the action of
C_{\mu}
on
H_{2\lambda}

In particular we have

(82)

\displaystyle C_{\mu}\cdot H_{\lambda}=\gamma H_{2\mu}\pi(H_{2\mu}^{*}H_{2\lambda})_{0}+(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})\pi((U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*}H_{2\lambda})_{0}.

More generally

(83)

\displaystyle C_{\mu}\cdot(u\cdot H_{2\lambda})=\gamma H_{2\mu}\pi(H_{2\mu}^{*}(u\cdot H_{2\lambda}))_{0}+\sum_{u\in U^{-}_{+}}(u\cdot H_{2\mu})\pi((U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*})(u\cdot H_{2\lambda}))_{0}

where the sum is over weight vectors
u
in
U^{-}_{+}
.
By Lemma
5.7
, if
|\mu|\geq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
then
\pi(H_{2\mu}^{*}H_{2\lambda})=\pi((U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*})H_{2\lambda})=0
.
Hence
C_{\mu}\cdot H_{2\lambda}=0
for
|\mu|\geq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
as desired.

Recall that
C_{\mu}
acts as the identity on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cdot H_{2\mu}
. Hence
C_{\mu}\cdot H_{2\mu}=H_{2\mu}
.
Hence by (
82
),
\gamma H_{2\mu}(\pi(H_{2\mu}^{*}H_{2\mu}))_{0}=H_{2\mu}
. (Note here we are taking into account that
U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}
is a sum of terms of weight strictly less than
2\mu
so have no contribution to
C_{\lambda}\cdot H_{2\mu})
.
Recall the definition of the bilinear form

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
right before Lemma
5.3
. In particular, this is a bilinear form on
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}\times\mathcal{P}_{\theta}
defined by

\langle d,p\rangle=\pi(dp)_{0}
.
Hence

C_{\mu}\cdot H_{2\mu}=H_{2\mu}=\gamma H_{2\mu}(\langle H_{2m}^{*},H_{2\mu}\rangle))
. Thus
\gamma=\langle H_{2\mu}^{*},H_{2\mu}\rangle^{-1}
.

∎

9.2.
Realization as polynomials

We start with the twisting relation between elements in
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
. These relationships will be key to taking products of Capelli operators. Much of the computations involve vector subspaces of
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
and vector subspaces of
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\mu\\
|\nu|=|\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}{\rm\hskip 10.00002ptand\hskip 10.00002pt}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\mu\\
|\nu|=|\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}

Here, the inequality
\nu<\mu
means that
\mu-\nu\in Q^{+}_{N}
. An equality such as
|\nu|=|\mu|
ensures that the entire subspace of
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
is of degree
|\mu|
and thus sits inside
\mathcal{J}_{|\mu|}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})
. A similar result holds for the subspaces of
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
.

Recall the relation described in Theorem
5.1
.
The next lemma provides a version of this relation using subspaces as described above.

Lemma 9.2
.

For all
a,b,e,
and
f
, the relation from Theorem
5.1
satisfies

(84)

\displaystyle d_{ab}x_{ef}-q^{(\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b},\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f})}x_{ef}d_{ab}\in\ q^{-\delta_{ef}}\delta_{ae}\delta_{bf}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Note that the weight of
d_{ab}
is
-\epsilon_{a}-\epsilon_{b}
and the weight of
x_{ef}
is
\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f}
. Hence the exponent of
q
preceding
x_{ef}d_{ab}
satisfies
\delta_{af}+\delta_{ae}+\delta_{bf}+\delta_{be}=(\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b},\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f}).

By
[
20
]
, Lemma 5.4,

\displaystyle F_{r}\cdot x_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ir}q^{-\delta_{rj}+\delta_{r,j-1}}x_{i+j,j}+\delta_{jr}x_{i,j+1}

\displaystyle E_{r}\cdot d_{ij}

\displaystyle=-(q^{-1}\delta_{ir}d_{i+1,j}+q^{1-\delta_{ri}+\delta_{r,i-1}}\delta_{jr}d_{i,j+1}).

Note that if
F_{r}\cdot x_{ij}\neq 0
, then the subscripts of
x_{ij}
increase upon application of
F_{r}
. Similarly, if
E_{r}\cdot d_{ij}\neq 0
, the subscripts of
d_{ij}
increase upon application of
E_{r}
. Therefore

\displaystyle d_{ab}x_{ef}-q^{(\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b},\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f})}x_{ef}d_{ab}\in\ q^{-\delta_{ef}}\delta_{ae}\delta_{bf}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}.

as desired.
∎

The next lemma gives applications of the relation in Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Given a weight vector
X_{2\gamma}
of weight
2\gamma
in
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
and a weight vector
D_{-2\mu}
of weight
-2\mu
in
\mathscr{D}_{\theta}
, we have

\displaystyle{D}_{-2\mu}{X}_{2\gamma}-q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}{X}_{2\gamma}{D}_{-2\mu}\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|\gamma|\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\mu\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}+\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})

Proof.

Consider first a term of the form
d_{e_{s},f_{s}}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}
. Using (
84
) as the term
d_{e_{s},f_{s}}
is moved to the right gives us

\displaystyle d_{e_{s},f_{s}}x_{g_{1},j_{1}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}

\displaystyle=q^{(\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}},\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{g_{2}})}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}d_{e_{s},f_{s}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}+\mathbb{C}(q)x_{g_{2},h_{2}}

\displaystyle=q^{(\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}},\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}+\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}})}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}d_{e_{s},f_{s}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}+\mathbb{C}(q)x_{g_{2},h_{2}}+\mathbb{C}(q)x_{g_{1},h_{1}}

From the relations satisfied by the generators for
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
(see Theorem
3.1
and formula (
7
)) we see that
(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\gamma}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\lambda}=(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\gamma+2\lambda}
. Similarly
(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\lambda}=(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\gamma-2\lambda^{\prime}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}+\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}}\\
|\nu|=4\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}\

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}

is spanned by linearly independent vectors of the form
d_{ef}
with weight
\nu^{\prime}=\epsilon_{e}+\epsilon_{f}
which is strictly less than
\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}
. Applying
(
84
) results in

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\leq\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}}\\
|\gamma|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{-\gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}+\mathbb{C}(q).

Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\leq\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}+\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}}\\
|\nu|=4\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}.

Therefore

\displaystyle d_{e_{s},f_{s}}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}-q^{(\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}},\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}+\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}})}x_{g_{1},h_{1}}x_{g_{2},h_{2}}d_{e_{s},f_{s}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\leq\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}+\epsilon_{g_{2}}+\epsilon_{h_{2}}\\
|\nu|=4\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\epsilon_{e_{s}}+\epsilon_{f_{s}}\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\epsilon_{g_{1}}+\epsilon_{h_{1}}\\
|\nu|=2\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}.

Note that this final term is in
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})
while the other terms are in
\mathcal{J}_{3}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})
.

We now turn our attention in applying such computations to the main assertion of the lemma. Since
D_{-2\mu}
is a weight vector, it can be written as a sum of products
d_{e_{1},f_{1}}d_{e_{2},f_{2}}\dots d_{e_{s},f_{s}}
and each summand has the same terms with the only difference being reordering. Moreover, since the weight of
d_{ab}
is
-\epsilon_{a}-\epsilon_{b}
, the weight
-2\mu
of
D_{-2\mu}
equals
\sum_{i}^{s}{-\epsilon_{e_{i}}-\epsilon_{f_{i}}}.
A similar analysis applies to
X_{2\gamma}
using elements
x_{ab}
instead of
d_{ab}
.
Repeated applications of (
9.2
), moving one term of the form
d_{e_{i},f_{i}}
to the right after the previous one, yields the desired formula.

The argument for the “moreover” part is similar. Start with a weight vector
D
of weight
-\nu
in the vector space
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}
and a weight vector
X
of weight
\nu^{\prime}
in the vector space
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}
. Repeated applications of (
84
) yields

\displaystyle DX-q^{(2\nu,2\nu^{\prime})}XD\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda^{\prime}<\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\lambda^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda<\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\lambda}+\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})

Since

\displaystyle XD\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}

it follows that

\displaystyle DX\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}+\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})

This holds for all weight vectors
D\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}
and
X\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}
.
∎

Note that an obvious application of Lemma
9.3
is to
D_{-2\mu}=H^{*}_{2\mu}
and
X_{2\gamma}=H_{2\gamma}
. In this case, we get

(85)

\displaystyle{H}_{2\mu}^{*}{H}_{2\gamma}-q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}{H}_{2\gamma}{H}_{2\mu}^{*}\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\mu\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

The next lemma gives another formulation for the Capelli operators described in Lemma
9.1
.

Lemma 9.4
.

The Capelli operator
C_{\mu}
for
\mu\in\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}
satisfies

\displaystyle C_{\mu}-\langle H_{2\mu}^{*},H_{2\mu}\rangle^{-1}H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\mu}\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
9.1
that the vector
C_{\mu}
is an element of

\displaystyle\langle H_{2\mu}^{*},H_{2\mu}\rangle^{-1}H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\mu}+(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})(U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*})

Recall that
{H}_{2\mu}^{*}
is a lowest weight vector and equals a sum of products
d_{e_{1},f_{1}}d_{e_{2},f_{2}}\dots d_{e_{s},f_{s}}
. Moreover, each summand has the same terms with the only difference being reordering. Note that the weight of
d_{ab}
is
-\epsilon_{a}-\epsilon_{b}
. Hence, the weight
-2\mu
of
{H}_{2\mu}^{*}
is equal to

-2\mu_{1}\hat{\eta}_{1}-\cdots-2\mu_{j}\hat{\eta}_{j}=\sum_{i}^{s}{-\epsilon_{e_{i}}-\epsilon_{f_{i}}}.
Similarly,
{H}_{2\mu}
is a highest weight vector equal to a a sum of the same products except that each
d_{e_{i},f_{i}}
is replaced with
x_{e_{i},f_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,s
and so the weight is
2\mu
instead of
-2\mu
.

By definition of
H_{2\mu}
we must have
\mu\in\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}
. Recall that
\nu<2\mu
means that
2\mu-\nu\in Q^{+}_{N}
. Taking the restricted version of
2\mu-\nu
means that

2\mu-\tilde{\nu}\in Q^{+}_{\Sigma}
. As explained in Section
7.3
,
Q_{\Sigma}\cap w_{0}\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}=0
. Since
w_{0}Q_{\Sigma}=Q_{\Sigma}
, we also have
Q_{\Sigma}\cap\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}=0
. Hence

\displaystyle H_{2\mu}H^{*}_{2\lambda}\notin\sum_{\nu<2\mu,\nu^{\prime}<2\lambda}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}.

for any
\mu,\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}_{\Sigma}
.

Contributions to
(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})
take the form of repeated applications of generators
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
to
H_{2\mu}
as described in Lemma
9.3
.
Since
H_{2\mu}\in(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\mu}
we see that
(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})\subseteq\sum_{\nu<2\mu}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}
.
Note that all these contributions must lie in degree
|2\mu|
since the action of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
on
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
preserves degree. Hence

(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}.

The same reasoning yields

\displaystyle(U^{+}_{+}\cdot H^{*}_{2\mu})\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}.

Putting these two inclusions together yields

\displaystyle(U^{-}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu})(U^{+}_{+}\cdot H_{2\mu}^{*})\subset\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\mu\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}.

Since
C_{\mu}\in(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}
, we can just consider those summands with
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
in the above formula. The lemma follows.
∎

It is worth noting that the intersection of
\mathscr{PD}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}
with the space
\left(U^{-}\cdot H_{2\mu}\right)\left(U^{+}\cdot H^{*}_{2\mu}\right)
is one-dimensional.
Indeed, by Lemma
9.1
the Capelli operator
C_{\mu}
is a basis vector for this space. As a consequence, we have

(86)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2|\mu|}(\mathscr{PD}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})})\subseteq\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\mu}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}\neq 2\mu\\
|\mu^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\mu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{2|\mu|-2}(\mathscr{PD}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}).

Proposition 9.5
.

Consider a Capelli operator
C_{\mu}
of degree
2\mu
where

\displaystyle\mu=m_{1}\hat{\eta}_{1}+\cdots+m_{j}\hat{\eta}_{j}

with
|\mu|=\sum_{i=1}^{j}m_{j}j
. It follows that

\displaystyle C_{\mu}=a_{\mu,\mu}C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots C_{\hat{\eta}_{n}}^{\mu_{n}}+\sum_{|\mu^{\prime}|<|\mu|}a_{\mu^{\prime}}C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots C_{\hat{\eta}_{j}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{j}}+a_{0}.

where

a_{\mu,\mu}=q^{(2\mu,2\mu)}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}\langle H_{2\hat{\eta}_{i}},H_{2\hat{\eta}_{i}}^{*}\rangle^{-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}\right)
,
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{\prime}_{1}\hat{\eta}_{1}+\cdots+\mu^{\prime}\hat{\eta}_{j}
and every
a_{\mu^{\prime}}
and
a_{0}
are scalars

Proof.

Set
c_{\mu,\gamma}=\langle H_{2\mu}^{*},H_{2\mu}\rangle^{-1}\langle H_{2\gamma}^{*},H_{2\gamma}\rangle^{-1}
.
Using (
85
) we see that that

(87)

\displaystyle C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}

\displaystyle-c_{\mu,\gamma}{H}_{2\mu}H_{2\mu}^{*}{H}_{2\gamma}{H}_{2\gamma}^{*}\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda<2\gamma\\
|\lambda|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\lambda}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\lambda}.

Switching the order of
H_{2\mu}^{*}
and
H_{2\gamma}
using (
85
) gives us

\displaystyle C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}

\displaystyle-c_{\mu,\gamma}q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}{H}_{2\mu}{H}_{2\gamma}H_{2\mu}^{*}{H}_{2\gamma}^{*}\in{H}_{2\mu}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\mu\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}\right){H}_{2\gamma}^{*}+{H}_{2\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})){H}_{2\gamma}^{*}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda<2\mu\\
|\lambda|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\lambda}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\lambda}.

Note that
{H}_{2\mu}(\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})){H}_{2\gamma}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}.

Also

\displaystyle H_{2\mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda<2\gamma\\
|\lambda|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\lambda}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu+\nu^{\prime}<2\mu+2\gamma\\
|\nu+\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu+2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu+\nu^{\prime}}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda^{\prime}<2\mu\\
|\lambda^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\lambda^{\prime}}H_{2\gamma}^{*}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu+\nu^{\prime}<2\mu+2\gamma\\
|\nu+\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu+2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu-\nu^{\prime}}.

Hence

(88)

\displaystyle H_{2\mu}(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\gamma\\
|\nu|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\mu\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}})H_{2\gamma}^{*}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu+\nu^{\prime}<2\mu+2\gamma\\
|\nu+\nu^{\prime}|=|2\mu+2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu+\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu-\nu^{\prime}}.

We now look at the other terms that show up in the formula for
C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}
. By Lemma
9.3
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}+\mathcal{J}_{|2\mu|+|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

Therefore

\displaystyle(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu})(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<2\mu\\
|\nu|=|2\mu|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<2\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|2\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

From the relations satisfied by the generators for
\mathscr{P}_{\theta}
(see Theorem
3.1
and formula (
7
)) we see that
(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu^{\prime}}=(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{\nu+\nu^{\prime}}
. Similarly
(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu^{\prime}}=(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-\nu-\nu^{\prime}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu<\mu\\
|\nu|=|\mu|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu^{\prime}<\gamma\\
|\nu^{\prime}|=|\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\nu}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\nu}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\nu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{|\mu|+|\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})

\displaystyle\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu+\nu^{\prime}<\mu+\gamma\\
|\nu+\nu^{\prime}|=|\mu+\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\nu+2\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\nu-2\nu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

This formula combined with (
87
) and (
88
) gives us

\displaystyle C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}

\displaystyle-c_{\mu,\gamma}q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}({H}_{2\mu}{H}_{2\gamma})({H}^{*}_{2\mu}{H}_{2\gamma}^{*})\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu+\nu^{\prime}<\mu+\gamma\\
|\nu+\nu^{\prime}|=|\mu+\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\nu+2\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\nu-2\nu^{\prime}}+\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

By Proposition
4.3
, the elements
H_{2\mu}
and
H_{2\mu^{\prime}}
commute with each other and the same holds for
H_{2\mu}^{*}
and
H_{2\gamma}^{*}
where
\mu,\gamma
are arbitrary weights. Moreover, by weight considerations discussed immediately following Proposition
4.3
,
H_{2\mu}H_{2\gamma}=H_{2\mu+2\gamma}
and
H^{*}_{2\mu}H_{2\gamma}^{*}=H^{*}_{2\mu+2\gamma}
. By Lemma
9.4
,
C_{\mu+\gamma}
is the unique (up to nonzero scalar) element of

\displaystyle H_{2\mu+2\gamma}H^{*}_{2\mu+2\gamma}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu+\nu^{\prime}<\mu+\gamma\\
|\nu+\nu^{\prime}|=|\mu+\gamma|\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{P}_{\theta})_{2\nu+2\nu^{\prime}}(\mathscr{D}_{\theta})_{-2\nu-2\nu^{\prime}}.

Hence

\displaystyle C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}-c_{\mu,\gamma}q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}C_{\mu+\gamma}\in\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}).

Restricting our attention to
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-invariant elements gives us

(89)

\displaystyle C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}-c_{\mu,\gamma}q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}C_{\mu+\gamma}\in\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}^{(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}).

Set
\mu=m_{1}\hat{\eta}_{1}+\cdots+m_{j}\hat{\eta}_{j}
and
\mu^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}\hat{\eta}_{1}+\cdots+m^{\prime}_{j}\hat{\eta}_{j}
for
\mu^{\prime}\neq\mu
and
|\mu^{\prime}|\leq|\mu|
. Using the above analysis and the fact that
(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta})^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}
is just the sum of one-dimensional subspaces of Capelli operators, we get

\displaystyle C_{\mu}-a_{\mu,\mu}C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots C_{\hat{\eta}_{j}}^{\mu_{j}}\in\sum_{|\mu^{\prime}|<|\mu|}\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\mu^{\prime}}

where
a_{\mu,\mu}=q^{(2\mu,2\mu)}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}\langle H_{2\hat{\eta}_{i}},H_{2\hat{\eta}_{i}}^{*}\rangle^{-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}\right)
.
Indeed the right hand side is the contribution to the lower degree terms in
\mathcal{J}_{2|2\mu|+2|2\gamma|-2}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})})
.
Continuing this process yields

\displaystyle C_{\mu}=a_{\mu,\mu}C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots C_{\hat{\eta}_{j}}^{\mu_{j}}+\sum_{|\mu^{\prime}|<|\mu|}a_{\mu^{\prime}}C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots C_{\hat{\eta}_{j}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{j}}+a_{0}

where each
a_{\mu^{\prime}}
and
a_{0}
are scalars. Thus the proof of the proposition is complete.
∎

Since
c_{\mu,\gamma}=c_{\gamma,\mu}
and
q^{(2\mu,2\gamma)}=q^{(2\gamma,2\mu)}
, the reader can follow the calculations in the previous proposition to conclude that
C_{\mu}C_{\gamma}=C_{\gamma}C_{\mu}
for all choices of
\mu
and
\gamma
, Hence the subalgebra generated by
C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}},\dots,C_{\hat{\eta}_{j}}
is commutative.

9.3.
The center and Capelli operators

Recall the definition of the subring
Z
generated by the central elements
\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{n}
living inside
U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
from Section
8.4
.
The next theorem relates this subalgebra
Z
of the center to the algebra of Capelli operators via the mapping
\Upsilon
of Corollary
7.5
.

Theorem 9.6
.

The
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-module algebra map
\Upsilon
defines an algebra isomorphism from the polynomial subring
Z
of
Z(U^{2}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
to the algebra coinciding with the vector space spanned by the Capelli operators. Moreover,
\deg\Upsilon(\hat{z}_{r})=2r
for
r=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

Set
c_{r}=\Upsilon(\hat{z}_{r})
for
r=1,\dots,n
. By Corollary
8.8
, the
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
generate the polynomial ring in
\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}
isomorphic to
Z
via
\Upsilon
. Also,
\deg c_{r}\leq 2r
for each
r
. In terms of the degree filtration
\mathcal{J}
, this means that
c_{r}\in\mathcal{J}_{2r}(\mathscr{PD}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})})
.

We argue by induction on
j
that

(90)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)[c_{1},\dots,c_{j}]=\mathbb{C}(q)[C_{\hat{\eta}_{1}},\dots,C_{\hat{\eta}_{j}}]

for
j=0,\dots,n
and also show that
\deg c_{j}=2j
for
j=1,\dots,n
.
Note that the equality of algebras holds for
j=0
simply because both sides of (
90
) are just the scalars. Now assume that (
90
) is true for some
j
satisfying
j>0
. By Corollary
8.8
,
\mathbb{C}(q)[c_{1},\dots,c_{n}]
is a polynomial ring with
n
variables. In particular, the elements
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
are algebraically independent. Hence

c_{j+1}\notin\mathbb{C}(q)[c_{1},\dots,c_{j}]
.

Recall that the elements
\hat{z}_{r},r=1,\dots,n
are in the center of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Also, by the definition of the Capelli operators (see the discussion preceding Lemma
9.1
),
\deg C_{\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}=2|\hat{\eta}_{j+1}|=2j+2.
By Corollary
7.5
,
c_{r}\in\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}

for each
r
. It follows that
c_{j+1}
is an element of

(91)

\displaystyle c_{j+1}\in\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}+\sum_{|\mu|=j+1,\mu\neq\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\mu}+\mathcal{J}_{2j}(\mathscr{PD}_{\theta}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}).

By Lemma
9.1
, each of the Capelli operators in the sum
\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}+\sum_{|\mu|=j+1,\mu\neq\hat{\eta}_{j+1}}\mathbb{C}(q)C_{\mu}
has degree
2\mu=2j+2
. Furthermore, this is also true for the entire sum because each Capelli operator
C_{\mu}
belongs to a subspace of the form
(U^{-}H_{\mu})(U^{+}H_{\mu}^{*})
of degree
2\mu
, for distinct values of
\mu
. Hence
\deg(c_{j+1})=2(j+1)
.

Consider a term of the form
C_{\mu}
where
|\mu|=2j+2,\mu\neq\hat{\eta}_{j+1}
. Since
C_{\mu}
has degree
2j+2
but
\mu\neq\hat{\eta}_{j+1}
, we must have

\displaystyle\mu=m_{1}\hat{\eta}_{1}+\cdots+m_{j}\hat{\eta}_{j}

with
